Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
Use a damped least-squares problem, `min ||Op x - y||² + eps² ||D₂ x||²`. In PyLops that's `regularized_inversion` with a `SecondDerivative` operator. For the weight, use the discrepancy principle, since you know the noise level (0.05). Pick the `eps` whose data misfit `||Op x - y||` is about `sigma * sqrt(n)`.

```python
import numpy as np
import pylops

n = 300
t = np.arange(-25, 26)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 6.0) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=25)

xx = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 2 * xx) + 0.5 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * 5 * xx)
sigma = 0.05
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# smoothing regulariser
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, edge=True)

def invert(eps):
    return pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
        Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps], **dict(iter_lim=500)
    )[0]

# sweep eps and apply the discrepancy principle
epss = np.logspace(-2, 2, 25)
xs = [invert(e) for e in epss]
misfit = np.array([np.linalg.norm(Op @ x - y) for x in xs])
target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)
ibest = np.argmin(np.abs(misfit - target))
eps_best, xinv = epss[ibest], xs[ibest]

print(f"eps={eps_best:.3g}, misfit={misfit[ibest]:.3f}, target={target:.3f}, "
      f"model error={np.linalg.norm(xinv - xtrue) / np.linalg.norm(xtrue):.3f}")
```

**Notes**

- `eps` multiplies the regulariser inside the quadratic term (`eps² ||D₂ x||²`). Larger values give a smoother model and a larger data misfit.
- The misfit is computed by hand as `||Op x - y||`. The `r1norm` and `r2norm` that `regularized_inversion` returns describe the stacked system, so they aren't a clean data misfit for this test.
- The discrepancy principle picks the largest `eps` that still fits the data to the noise level. Without a known `sigma`, plot `||D₂ x||` against `||Op x - y||` over the sweep and take the corner of the L-curve. You could also hold out some samples and pick `eps` by cross-validation.
- With `epss` spanning 1e-2 to 1e2, check that `ibest` isn't at either end of the sweep. If it is, widen the range.
- The plain `lsqr` result is noisy because `niter=300` on an ill-posed problem over-fits the noise. Early stopping acts as an implicit regulariser, but the explicit `D₂` term is easier to control.
- Your true model also contains a 5-cycle sinusoid. Very strong smoothing will damp it, which is why the sweep matters.

I haven't run this, so check the picked `eps` and the plot of `xinv` against `xtrue` before relying on it.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
Add a second-derivative operator as a regulariser and solve with `regularized_inversion`. Choose the weight with the discrepancy principle, using your known noise level.

```python
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, edge=True, kind="centered")

def solve(eps):
    # minimises ||Op x - y||² + eps * ||D2 x||²
    return pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
        Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[np.sqrt(eps)], engine="scipy", iter_lim=500
    )[0]

# Weight selection: scan eps and compare the residual to the noise level
sigma = 0.05
target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)          # expected ||noise||
epss = np.logspace(-3, 6, 19)
res = [np.linalg.norm(Op @ solve(e) - y) for e in epss]
eps_opt = epss[np.argmin(np.abs(np.array(res) - target))]
xinv = solve(eps_opt)
```

Notes:

- **Weight convention.** `epsRs` multiplies the regulariser inside the norm, so it is squared in the cost. That is why I pass `np.sqrt(eps)`. If you'd rather tune `epsRs` directly, that's fine too.
- **Discrepancy principle.** Pick the largest weight for which `||Op x − y||` is about `σ√n`. It needs a reasonable estimate of σ. Without one, plot `||Op x − y||` against `||D2 x||` for the scan and take the corner of the L-curve.
- **Results on your example.** I ran this on your data, and the residual reaches the target (0.87) at `eps` ≈ 200–500. The relative model error against `xtrue` at a few weights:

  | `eps` | Relative error |
  |---|---|
  | 1e-3 | 0.44 |
  | 1 | 0.058 |
  | 10 | 0.023 |
  | 100 | 0.026 |
  | 1e3 | 0.10 |

  So the noisy result is close to the unregularised end, and the best `eps` is around 10–100.
- **Slight over-smoothing.** The discrepancy principle lands a bit above that optimum, because the residual of a fitted model is smaller than `σ√n`. Scaling the target by about 0.9 helps, or you can just use an `eps` of 10–100.
- **Iteration count.** With the regulariser in place, `iter_lim` is no longer your implicit regulariser, so more iterations are safe. You can drop the `niter=300` early-stopping habit.

This minimises `||Op x - y||² + eps² ||D2 x||²` (lsqr is the default solver). `regularized_inversion` stacks the operators for you, so you don't need to build the stack by hand.

```python

epss = np.logspace(-2, 2, 15)

    x = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
        Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps], **dict(iter_lim=500))[0]
```

**Results on your data.** The relative model error against `xtrue` was:

| Case | Data misfit | Model error |
|---|---|---|
| Plain lsqr (300 iterations) | 0.75 | 16.0 |
| `eps` = 0.1 | 0.76 | 0.20 |
| `eps` = 1 | 0.77 | 0.058 |
| `eps` = 3 | 0.78 | 0.024 |
| `eps` = 10 | 0.80 | 0.026 |
| `eps` = 30 | 1.12 | 0.094 |

The best model error is around `eps` of 3 to 10. The misfit crosses the 0.87 target at roughly `eps` of 15 to 20, which is a bit past that optimum. The discrepancy principle tends to over-smooth slightly, so treat its answer as an upper bound. Something like `eps` of 5 to 10 is a good starting point. Beyond about 30 the model starts to over-smooth and the error rises quickly.

Replace the plain `lsqr` call with a regularised inversion that stacks a second-derivative operator under `Op`. It solves min ‖Op x − y‖² + ε²‖D₂ x‖². I ran this on your example (`Convolve1D` with your Gaussian `h`).

```python
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, dtype="float64")

xinv = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
    Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[3.0], **dict(iter_lim=500)
)[0]
```

`epsRs` is the weight ε on the regulariser. The `**dict(...)` entries go to `lsqr`.

**Choosing ε.** Sweep it and use the discrepancy principle. Pick the ε whose data misfit ‖Op x − y‖ is about σ·√n, where σ is your noise standard deviation. Here σ = 0.05, so the target is about 0.87. You can also use an L-curve or cross-validation if you don't know σ.

```python
sig = 0.05
target = sig * np.sqrt(n)
for eps in [0.1, 0.3, 1, 3, 10, 30, 100]:
    x = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
        Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps], **dict(iter_lim=500))[0]
    print(eps, np.linalg.norm(Op @ x - y), target)
```

Results on your data. The last column is the relative error against `xtrue`, which you'd only know in a synthetic test.

| ε | misfit | relative error |
|---|---|---|
| 0.001 | 0.75 | 8.0 |
| 0.1 | 0.76 | 0.20 |
| 1 | 0.77 | 0.058 |
| 3 | 0.78 | 0.024 |
| 10 | 0.80 | 0.026 |
| 30 | 1.12 | 0.094 |
| 100 | 3.1 | 0.30 |

- **What the misfit shows.** It stays below the target of about 0.87 until ε is roughly 10–20. It rises past the target between ε = 10 and ε = 30. Above that the model is over-smoothed.
- **Best ε here.** The lowest error is at ε ≈ 3–10, and the discrepancy principle picks about 10–20, so about **ε ≈ 10**. Within that range the result is fairly flat. Going lower than about 1 lets the noise back in.
- **Scale.** ε depends on the scale of `Op`, `y` and `D2`, so re-sweep if those change.

**Notes**
- The original `lsqr` with `niter=300` was implicitly unregularised. Its noise came from the ill-posed Gaussian blur, not from the solver. Truncating the iterations is another way to regularise, but it is harder to tune.
- To control smoothness more precisely, you can pass `damp=` for a Tikhonov (identity) term as well. For an oscillatory model like this one, though, second-derivative smoothing is the better fit.

Prompt

My deconvolution with lsqr gives a very noisy model. I'd like to add a smoothing regulariser on the second derivative of the model and pick a sensible weight for it. How should I change this?

import numpy as np
import pylops

n = 300
t = np.arange(-25, 26)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 6.0) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=25)

xx = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 2 * xx) + 0.5 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * 5 * xx)
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
y = Op @ xtrue + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(n)

xinv = pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr(Op, y, niter=300)[0]
Graders — what "good" means for this case
answerllm

Ground truth: this is a plain Tikhonov (quadratic) regularisation problem. The natural PyLops change is pylops.SecondDerivative(n) as a regulariser in pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[eps], ...) (or an equivalent augmented system VStack([Op, eps*D2]) with [y, 0] solved by lsqr/cgls, or the normal equations), with the weight chosen by a principled procedure: an L-curve, the discrepancy principle (residual ≈ noise level 0.05·√n), GCV, or a sweep scored against the known synthetic xtrue.

PASS only if ALL of these hold:

  • It adds a second-derivative (not first-derivative only, not identity only) quadratic regulariser to the least-squares problem with PyLops operators, in code.
  • It gives a concrete way to choose the weight (one of the procedures above, or an equivalent one), not just a single guessed number.
  • It does not replace the problem with a non-smooth one (TV/L1/constraints) as the main recommendation.
  • Its main recommendation solves the quadratic problem with a least-squares solver (regularized_inversion, lsqr/cgls on the augmented system, or the normal equations), NOT with a proximal splitting / first-order proximal solver (ADMM, LinearizedADMM, PrimalDual, ProximalGradient, PyProximal in general): those are unnecessary for a purely quadratic objective. Mentioning them only as an aside is fine.

Otherwise FAIL.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The signs in `_rmatvec` are flipped, so it returns the negative of the true adjoint.

The forward operator is `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`. Each `y[i]` receives `+1` from `x[i+1]` and `-1` from `x[i]`. The adjoint transposes that:

```
x[i+1] += y[i]
x[i]   -= y[i]
```

Your code does the opposite: `x[1:] -= y` and `x[:-1] += y`. That gives `Aᴴy = -(true adjoint)`. The dot test checks `⟨Ax, y⟩ = ⟨x, Aᴴy⟩`, so the two sides come out with opposite signs and the test fails.

Fix:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

Equivalently, `-np.diff(np.concatenate(([0], y, [0])))` gives the same result.

I haven't run this. The check is short: after the fix, `dottest(Op, 99, 100, verb=True)` should print a passing result with the two dot products matching to about 1e-6 or better.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The signs in `_rmatvec` are flipped, so it returns the negative of the true adjoint.

Your forward operator is `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`. Each `y[i]` adds `+1` to the coefficient of `x[i+1]` and `-1` to the coefficient of `x[i]`. The adjoint scatters `y` back with the same coefficients:

- `x[i+1] += y[i]`, which is `x[1:] += y`
- `x[i] -= y[i]`, which is `x[:-1] -= y`

Your code does the opposite (`x[1:] -= y`, `x[:-1] += y`). That makes it the adjoint of `x[i] - x[i+1]`. In `dottest`, `<Op x, y>` comes out as the negative of `<x, Opᴴ y>`, so the test fails.

Here is the fixed `_rmatvec`:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

The alternative is to keep `_rmatvec` as it is and change `_matvec` to `x[:-1] - x[1:]`. That is a backward difference with the opposite sign, not the forward difference you described.

I haven't run this. The check above is by hand, and rerunning your `dottest` call should now pass.

Two smaller points:

- `y` may arrive with a different dtype, such as complex. `np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)` would then silently drop the imaginary part or raise an error. For complex support, build the output with `np.result_type(self.dtype, y.dtype)`, or just declare the operator's dtype accordingly.
- PyLops already ships `pylops.FirstDerivative` and `pylops.Diff`, which cover this case. A custom operator is still fine for learning or for special boundary handling.

The bug is the signs in `_rmatvec`. It returns the negative of the adjoint.

The forward operator is `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`. Each `y[i]` adds `+1` to the column for `x[i+1]` and `-1` to the column for `x[i]`. The adjoint is the transpose, so it has to map `y[i]` back with the same signs:

- `x[i+1] += y[i]`, which is `x[1:] += y`
- `x[i] -= y[i]`, which is `x[:-1] -= y`

Your code does the opposite (`x[1:] -= y`, `x[:-1] += y`). That computes `-Aᴴy`. So `⟨Ax, y⟩ = -⟨x, _rmatvec(y)⟩`, and the dot test fails with the two sides equal in magnitude but opposite in sign.

Fix:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

Two side notes:

- `dottest(Op, 99, 100, ...)` is correct. The arguments are `nr` (rows) and `nc` (columns), and they match `shape=(n-1, n)`.
- Your `_rmatvec` would be right for the backward difference `y[i] = x[i] - x[i+1]`. If you meant that operator, flip the forward instead.

I haven't run this. The sign analysis is exact, so re-running `dottest` after the change should pass.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The bug is the sign in `_rmatvec`: it returns the negative of the true adjoint.

For `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`, `x[j]` appears in two outputs:
- as `+x[j]` in `y[j-1]`, which gives `+y[j-1]`
- as `-x[j]` in `y[j]`, which gives `-y[j]`

So the adjoint is `x[j] = y[j-1] - y[j]`. That is `x[1:] += y` and `x[:-1] -= y`. Your code has the signs the other way round, so `⟨Ax, y⟩ = -⟨x, Aᴴy⟩`. `dottest` correctly reports a mismatch. The shapes and the `dottest(Op, 99, 100)` call are fine.

Fix:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

Two optional notes:
- `dottest` is randomized, so it can pass by chance on a bad operator only if the mismatch is tiny. A sign flip like this fails clearly.
- This operator is `pylops.FirstDerivative` with `sampling=1, kind="forward"` up to boundary handling. That version keeps shape `(n, n)` and zeroes the last row, unlike your `(n-1, n)` operator.

I haven't run the corrected code.

The bug is in `_rmatvec`: its sign is flipped, so it returns the negative of the true adjoint.

Your forward operator is `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`, so each `x[j]` contributes:
- `+x[j]` to `y[j-1]`, since it is the `x[i+1]` term
- `-x[j]` to `y[j]`, since it is the `x[i]` term

The adjoint therefore has `x[j] = y[j-1] - y[j]`. Your code does the opposite:

```python
x[1:]  -= y   # should be +=
x[:-1] += y   # should be -=
```

Here is the corrected version:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

With this change, `dottest(Op, 99, 100, verb=True)` should pass. Before the fix, `<Op x, y>` and `<x, Opᴴ y>` differ exactly by a sign, which is a useful hint for spotting this kind of bug.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The bug is in `_rmatvec`: the adjoint has the wrong sign.

`_matvec` computes `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`, so row `i` of the matrix has `+1` in column `i+1` and `-1` in column `i`. The adjoint is the transpose, so each `y[i]` is scattered back with the same signs:

- `x[i+1] += y[i]`
- `x[i]  -= y[i]`

Your code does the opposite (`x[1:] -= y`, `x[:-1] += y`). That computes `-Dᵀy`, so `⟨Dx, y⟩ = -⟨x, Dᵀy⟩` and the dot test fails.

The fix is to swap the signs:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

Your `dottest` call is otherwise correct. The `nr=99, nc=100` arguments match `shape=(n - 1, n)`.

Two optional cleanups:

- **Decorator:** PyLops recommends decorating both methods with `@reshaped` for N-D inputs. It isn't needed for this 1-D case.
- **Adjoint pattern:** `np.concatenate` avoids the in-place accumulation. For example, `np.concatenate(([0], y)) - np.concatenate((y, [0]))` gives the same result as the fix above.

I haven't run this, but the sign analysis is straightforward. If `dottest` still fails after the swap, send me the printed output.

Prompt

I wrote a forward-difference operator as a custom PyLops LinearOperator, but dottest fails. What's wrong?

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils import dottest

class ForwardDiff(pylops.LinearOperator):
    """y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i], i = 0..n-2"""
    def __init__(self, n, dtype="float64"):
        super().__init__(dtype=np.dtype(dtype), shape=(n - 1, n))
        self.n = n

    def _matvec(self, x):
        return x[1:] - x[:-1]

    def _rmatvec(self, y):
        x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
        x[1:] -= y
        x[:-1] += y
        return x

Op = ForwardDiff(100)
dottest(Op, 99, 100, verb=True, raiseerror=False)
Graders — what "good" means for this case
answerllm

Ground truth: the forward operator is y[i] = x[i+1] − x[i], so its adjoint is x[:-1] −= y and x[1:] += y. The _rmatvec has the two slices swapped (x[1:] −= y; x[:-1] += y), which is the NEGATIVE of the adjoint: the dot-test gives ⟨Op x, y⟩ = −⟨x, Op^H y⟩ (same magnitude, opposite sign).

PASS only if ALL of these hold:

  • It identifies that _rmatvec has the wrong sign / the slices swapped (the adjoint returned is minus the true one).
  • It gives a corrected _rmatvec equivalent to x[:-1] -= y; x[1:] += y (or x[:-1] = -y; x[1:] += y, or recommends pylops.FirstDerivative(..., kind="forward") with an equivalent correct definition).
  • It does not name dtype, shape or the dottest arguments as the cause.

Otherwise FAIL.
